## Document 316: How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

### Page 4

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

### Page 6

6

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

### Page 7

7

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And finally, just a few words about a larger subject, China.  
0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate. 
0:22:29 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Cynthia, for being with us today.  
0:22:32 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Thank you. 
0:22:33 - Emmanuel Laborde 
It is now time to conclude, and we welcome back Group Chief Economist Isabelle Mateos y Lago.  
It is time for our conclusion with Isabelle. Once again, just a final question, please. Could or should, I do not know which 
word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.  
0:23:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Isabelle. Thank you all for joining us today. Just a few words to remind you that additional economic 
research content can be found by scanning the QR code shown on your screen. Do not hesitate to use it with your 
smartphone. It is very easy. It was a pleasure to be with you today. Have a nice day and hope to see you so

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive 
outlook for the AUD

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

GBP/USD’s pre-Budget weakness sets an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure over 
the medium-term

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 351: Quantum dictionary, part 1_ Decoding the fundamentals From superposition to decoherence, we unpack the concepts and technologies shaping the future of quantum computing. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Quantum dictionary, part 1_ Decoding the fundamentals From superposition to decoherence, we unpack the concepts and technologies shaping the future of quantum computing. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13030420     1

Transformation 
Quantum dictionary, part 1: Decoding the fundamentals 
21 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• What is quantum computing? How does it work? What is a qubit? And when will quantum reach its full potential? Quantum 
computing challenges our intuition and our understanding of what computers can do. In this two-part dictionary series, we 
decode key terms behind what could be the next major technological revolution. 
• Qubits are the basic building blocks of quantum computers, harnessing properties such as superposition and entanglement to 
process information. However, qubits are highly sensitive to their surroundings where disturbances can lead to errors in 
quantum computation, making error correction a key challenge for building quantum computers. 
• There is no single approach to building a quantum computer. Researchers and companies are pursuing a range of qubit 
technologies, from neutral atoms and photons to spin, superconducting, topological and trapped-ion qubits, while quantum 
computing itself is likely to increasingly work alongside classical computing and AI.

First, let’s lay the groundwork… 
Quantum computing  
Quantum computing uses qubits and quantum properties such as superposition (defined later on) to perform complex 
calculations and solve certain problems faster than classical computers.1 From a practical perspective, quantum computing could 
be used to tackle problems related to optimization, machine learning, simulation and factorization across a range of sectors, 
including autos and logistics, financial services, energy and climate, health and life sciences and national security. 
Current quantum computers can perform ‘just’ thousands of quantum operations. As the technology advances from thousands to 
millions, billions and eventually trillions of operations, quantum computers could begin to have commercial applications.  
• Millions: Financial services and security could experience some of the first use cases, such as threat detection, fraud 
prevention, derivatives pricing, bond trading and arbitrage. 
• Billions: Quantum could help power AI through advanced data generation capabilities.  
• Trillions: Quantum chemistry applications could emerge, including drug discovery, battery simulation and materials 
discovery. 
These are the publicly known applications, according to Oxford Quantum Circuits (OQC). And as quantum computing capabilities 
improve, some of these use cases could emerge sooner than anticipated.  
Now, we’ll tackle the core concepts… 
Qubits 
Qubits (quantum bits) are a quantum computer’s basic unit of information. They can exhibit quantum properties such as 
superposition and entanglement (which we elaborate on below) — properties fundamental to quantum computing. 
Quantum gates manipulate the state of qubits, much like logic gates manipulate bits in classical computers. Despite the name, a 
quantum gate is not a physical object, but rather an operation applied to a quantum system that changes its state. 
From physical to logical qubits 
Physical qubits are the foundation of a quantum computer’s hardware. However, they are highly susceptible to error. To address 
this, multiple physical qubits can be structured together to encode a logical qubit, allowing errors to be detected and corrected

1 Schneider, J., & Smalley, I. (2025, June 10). What is quantum computing? IBM. 
I NSTI TUTE
Accessible version

### Page 2

2 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

and making the stored quantum information more reliable. In recent years, according to BofA Global Research, logical qubits 
have become a greater focus for quantum computing companies. 
Entanglement 
Entanglement is a condition where two or more qubits become linked so that their states are correlated, regardless of distance 
(Exhibit 1). It allows quantum computers to process information in ways that can be difficult or impossible for classical 
computers. 
So, how does it work? Suppose we have two photons that have opposite polarizations (the orientation of their light waves). 
Before measurement, neither has a definite polarization. When one is measured as horizontally polarized, the other will be 
vertically polarized when measured in the same basis, wherever it is. When measured separately, they appear to be random, but 
when compared, they are correlated (Exhibit 2). 
The challenges 
Once created, entanglement must be protected from decoherence (where interactions with the environment destroy quantum 
correlations). Maintaining entanglement over long distances is another challenge. Photons traveling through optical fiber can be 
absorbed or scattered, making long-distance quantum communication difficult. 2 Quantum repeaters aim to overcome this 
challenge by extending entanglement across a series of shorter links. 
Exhibit 1: Entanglement is a state where at least two qubits become 
correlated 
An infographic of entangled qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 2: Measuring one qubit in an entangled pair instantly 
determines the state of the other, no matter the distance 
An example of what entanglement means for qubit measurement

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Superposition 
Unlike a classical bit, which can be one of two states (0 or 1), superposition allows a quantum system to exist in multiple states 
or configurations at the same time (Exhibit 3). For example, a qubit can exist in the 0 state, the 1 state or any state that is a 
combination of 0 and 1.3  
A quantum system can maintain a superposition until it is observed or measured. Once measured, its superposition collapses into 
a single state. Another way of thinking about superposition is to imagine a sphere with the north pole representing zero and the 
south pole representing one. Other points on the surface of the sphere represent different superposition states. This allows us 
to: 1) represent complex forms of information; and 2) simulate atoms in a computer. 
Schrödinger’s cat 
In 1935, physicist Erwin Schrödinger proposed a thought experiment to illustrate the strange implications of quantum 
mechanics. In this scenario, a cat, a radioactive substance that decays very slowly, and a Geiger counter are put together in a 
sealed box. According to quantum mechanics, the system can be described as a superposition in which the cat is both alive and 
dead until the box is opened. Once the box is opened and the system is observed, the superposition collapses into a single 
outcome.

2 Rehman, M. (2026, May 11). What is Quantum Entanglement? Quantum Insider. 
3 Microsoft. (n.d.). Superposition.

### Page 3

21 October 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

Enabling parallel computation versus classical computing 
Superposition is a key attribute of quantum computing that allows for quantum parallelism, or the ability to perform multiple 
computations at the same time. Classical bits can only be in one of two states, 0 or 1. In contrast, a qubit can be in a 
superposition of 0 and 1, allowing a quantum computer to process multiple possible qubit states at once.4  
Exhibit 3: Superposition is the condition where a qubit can exist in a superposition of 0 and 1 
Illustrating the power of superposition

Source: BofA Global Research 
Note: A Bloch sphere is a geometric representation of qubit states. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Error rates and fidelity 
Fidelity is a measure of how closely a quantum state or operation matches what was intended (Exhibit 4). A higher fidelity 
generally means fewer errors. 
Physical qubits are prone to errors. One way to address this is to combine multiple physical qubits to create a more reliable 
logical qubit. Quantum error correction (QEC) allows errors in the physical qubits to be detected and corrected, helping protect 
the information stored in the logical qubit.

4 Ibid.

### Page 4

4 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 4: Fidelity measures how accurately a qubit retains its intended quantum state during computation 
Infographic showing the implications of high versus low fidelity

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
How does quantum error correction work? 
At its simplest, QEC relies on redundancy, or storing information across multiple qubits so that errors can be detected and 
corrected.  
Consider an example: suppose someone wants to send a message represented by 0. Instead of sending a single 0, the message 
could be repeated three times as 000, known as a codeword. If one bit were accidentally flipped from 0 to 1 during transmission, 
the recipient might receive 010. Because two of the three bits are still 0, the recipient could infer that the intended message 
was 000. 
However, if two bits flipped, producing 110 or 011, the recipient could infer the wrong message. If all three flipped to 111, the 
recipient would have no way to know from the received codeword that errors had occurred.5 This approach is known as a 
repetition code and illustrates how redundancy can help protect information from errors.  
Repetition codes are a simple form of error correction that can address bit-flip errors, where a 0 changes to a 1 or vice versa. 
More sophisticated QEC codes are needed to address other quantum errors, such as phase flips.6 Shor’s code, developed by 
American theoretical computer scientist Peter Shor, uses nine physical qubits to encode one logical qubit and can correct one 
bit-flip error or one phase flip error, but it can’t correct both types of errors at the same time. Meanwhile, Steane’s seven-qubit 
code, developed by Oxford physicist Andrew Steane, can also correct both types of errors. It has the advantage of being fault-
tolerant, meaning the error-correction process itself does not introduce additional errors.7 
At what error level might we start seeing quantum advantage? 
Some classical computing systems today operate with extremely high reliability, often described as having "17 nines" of fidelity 
(99.99999999999999999%). By comparison, quantum computers currently achieve fidelity rates of around 99.9%. Gerald 
Mullally, CEO of OQC, has argued that quantum systems would need to add roughly three more nines of fidelity to achieve 
commercial relevance, or quantum advantage. 
Noise and decoherence 
Noise refers to unwanted disturbances that affect quantum systems and can lead to errors in computations (Exhibit 5). It can 
come from sources such as interactions with the environment, electromagnetic interference and temperature fluctuations. 
Coherence is a quantum system’s ability to maintain its quantum properties, including superposition and entanglement. When 
coherence is lost through interactions with the environment, the process is known as “decoherence,” causing the system to 
behave more classically (Exhibit 6).8

5 Microsoft Ignite. (n.d.) Introduction to quantum error correction. 
6 Ibid. 
7 Ibid. 
8 Quandela. (n.d.) Quantum Decoherence.

### Page 5

21 October 2026    5

I NSTI TUTE

To fully control qubits for calculations and information transfer, they need to be carefully protected from their surroundings. 
External factors like noise, energy, changes in Earth’s magnetic field, radiation from Wi-Fi or mobile phones or cosmic rays can 
interfere with the process. Even small disturbances can lead to decoherence, making it difficult to develop large-scale, fault-
tolerant quantum computers.9 
According to QuEra Computing, quantum noise can be mitigated through physical isolation of qubits, the development of more 
precise control techniques and quantum error correction, which helps detect and correct errors in physical qubits.  
Exhibit 5: Noise refers to unwanted disturbances, e.g., interactions 
with the environment, that lead to errors in quantum computation 
An infographic explaining quantum noise

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 6: Decoherence is when qubits lose their key quantum 
properties, superposition and entanglement 
An infographic of decoherence

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Quantum processing unit (QPU) 
Referred to as the brain of the quantum computer, the QPU is the core hardware where qubits reside and where quantum 
computations take place. QPUs rely on quantum behaviors like superposition and entanglement to tackle problems in areas such 
as cryptography, simulation and optimization. Just as a CPU (central processing unit) in classical computing contains multiple 
components, a QPU includes quantum gates (operations that manipulate qubits in a controlled way, similar to logic gates in 
classical computing) and interconnects, which enable communication between qubits and other quantum processors.10 
Next, we’ll look at broader computing architecture… 
Heterogeneous computing  
Heterogeneous computing refers to a future computing landscape likely combining classical and quantum computing, potentially 
unlocking more powerful, faster and cheaper compute and helping solve problems that classical computers cannot. According to 
BofA Global Research, AI and quantum computing could also converge and complement each other, enabling more 
transformative use cases. 
Quantum modality  
A quantum modality refers to the type of physical technology used to create and manipulate qubits. We discuss the modalities in 
greater depth in the following section. 
Lastly, we outline qubit technologies… 
Neutral atom qubit 
A quantum modality that uses individual atoms with no electrical charge as qubits (Exhibit 7). The atoms are held in place using 
focused lasers known as optical tweezers. Neutral atom qubit systems do not require atoms to be ionized, which means fewer 
energy constraints and potentially simpler cooling requirements. Atoms such as rubidium or cesium are typically used. The qubits 
are manipulated by changing the energy levels or positions of the trapped atoms using laser pulses.

9 QuEra. (n.d.) Quantum Noise. 
10 SpinQ. (2025, January 16). What does a Quantum Computer Look Like? Explain Key Parts.

### Page 6

6 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 7: A quantum modality where atoms with a net electrical charge of zero are the foundation for qubits 
Key features of neutral atoms

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Photonic qubit 
A quantum modality that uses particles of light, known as photons, to carry quantum information (Exhibit 8).11 Optical 
components such as beam splitters, which divide light along different paths, and phase shifters, which change the phase of light, 
can be used to perform quantum operations. Photonic quantum computers can use integrated photonic chips to manipulate 
photons and enable quantum logic. 
Exhibit 8: A quantum modality relying on the quantum properties of light 
Key features of photonic qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

11 Moore, J. (2025, March 28). The 6 different types of quantum computing technology. TechTarget.

### Page 7

21 October 2026    7

I NSTI TUTE

Spin qubit 
A quantum modality that uses the intrinsic spin of electrons or nuclei in solid-state materials, such as silicon or diamond. These 
spins can be manipulated using magnetic or electric fields, and their states are read using optical or charge-sensing techniques. 
Spin qubits are particularly appealing because they can leverage existing CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor; a 
technology to build processors) manufacturing infrastructure, making it easier to integrate them with conventional processors. 
Superconducting qubit 
A quantum modality in which electronic circuits create qubits. They are built using Josephson junctions, where two 
superconductors are separated by a thin insulating layer. Microwave pulses manipulate the qubits’ quantum states to run 
computations.12  
Specifically, a resistance-free current oscillates back and forth around a quantum loop. An injected microwave signal excites the 
current into superposition states.13 To enable this process, superconducting materials are cooled to near absolute zero (around 
10-20 millikelvin) using dilution refrigerators, helping reduce thermal noise and maintain coherence. 
Topological qubit 
A quantum modality that aims to encode quantum information using topological properties rather than relying only on individual 
particle states (Exhibit 9). One way to think about this is through the paths certain quasiparticles take as they move around one 
another, creating “braids” in space-time. Because the information can depend on the overall pattern of these braids rather than 
small details of their paths, the approach is designed to be more resilient to certain disturbances. 
Exhibit 9: Resilient to small perturbations but requires special quasiparticles not yet been conclusively demonstrated in nature 
An infographic of topological qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Trapped-ion qubit  
A quantum modality in which ions (electrically charged atoms) are held in place using electromagnetic fields. These ions act as 
the qubits and are controlled by the system with tightly focused laser beams.14 The qubits are held inside ultra-high vacuum 
(UHV) chambers.

12 Ibid. 
13 Simon Benjamin 
14 Moore, J. (2025, March 28). The 6 different types of quantum computing technology. TechTarget.

French government bond spreads continue to widen versus Germany on the
ﬁscal situation and more recently the protests
France remains one of the focal issues in the eurozone. The spread

of French government bonds over Germany has come under
renewed widening pressure against the backdrop of violent protests
grabbing the headlines.
The protests inject more uncertainty into the dynamics ahead of
the presidential elections. On the right, Le Pen has jumped on public
unrest to promote her law-and-order platform. Others have shifted
the blame to the left. And indeed, if betting markets are to be
trusted, the beneﬁciaries appear to be the right and centre

candidates, while on the far left, Mélenchon’s chances of winning

More importantly, the protests highlight that any government will

Tuesday will likely see more headlines around the 2027 budget, as
Parliament is slated for a ﬁrst formal vote on the revenue section of
the ﬁnance bill. From a ﬂurry of proposed amendments, what

remains is tax revenues being pared back and tax breaks expanded.
It appears parties are still preoccupied with positioning themselves
for the upcoming elections rather than tackling the ﬁscal issue.
It is not the end of the debate, though. Following the revenue
section is the social security budget and then the expenditure part
of the ﬁnance bill. All bills will be passed on to the Senate and
eventually back to Parliament for a ﬁnal vote. That is, in theory. The

government still has the option to bypass Parliament to push
through its ﬁscal consolidation plans, though at the risk of a no-
conﬁdence vote.
For now, a lack of will to make unpleasant but needed choices is
likely to keep spreads over Bunds on a path towards testing recent

the sovereign debt crisis. We have been looking for the spread to

up, and despite some dovish ECB remarks around the tightening of
ﬁnancial conditions, energy market pressures are tying the ECB’s

scheduled rating review from Moody’s.

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2. 2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%) . 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.5% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4.25% - 4.50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y in Q3 2026 (1.2% q/q) after 4.3% y/y in Q2 and 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a 
whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth remains characterised by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports 
and the fragility of sectors that rely on domestic demand has widened this year. Retail sales barely increased in the first nine months of 2026, 
the crisis in the property sector has continued and household confidence remains low. Fiscal policy measures aimed at supporting investment 
and private consumption will increase over the last months of the year . CPI inflation has rebounded slightly since last March, mostly due to 
the rise in global energy prices, as well as due to the increase in electronic good prices and anti-involution measures implemented by the 
authorities. Deflationary pressures persist, however, given weakness in domestic demand.

EUROZONE 
Eurozone growth is expected to slow to 1.1% in 2026 (from 1.3% in 2025), before strengthening to 1.5% in 2027, supported by higher defence 
and infrastructure spending as well as the global AI capex cycle. The economy has proven resilient to the energy shock, notab ly thanks to the 
increasingly tangible impact of G erman defence spending on the real economy. Elevated energy prices in the autumn (especially for natural 
gas), combined with resilient growth, are set to accelerate inflation to a peak around the turn of the year. Annual average i nflation is thus 
expected to reach  3.1% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), before easing to 3.0% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, we 
anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause . That said, 
stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

### Page 3

FRANCE 
GDP growth remained low in Q2 (0% after -0.2% in Q1) as a result of deteriorated agricultural and construction output, as well as higher 
inflation (2.5% in 2026 according to our forecast, after 0.9% in 2025) . In 2026, GDP growth should  reach 0.5% (after 0.9% in 2025), driven 
mainly by exports.

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would improve slightly to 1.3% in 2026 and remain at this level in 2027  (1.2% in 2025). The economy has so far weathered 
both the energy price shock and domestic political volatility. That said, growth is expected to decelerate in the second half  of the year, while 
inflation is set to remain stubbornly above target (averag ing 3.2% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027), driven by higher energy prices and mounting 
second-round effects. In response, we expect the BoE to deliver a single insurance hike in November 2026, bringing the Bank Rate to 4.00%. 
The BoE is then expected to resume easing in H2 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank Rate to 3.50% by end-year.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 1.0% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.2% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy  and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and 30-
year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end -2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

## Document 407: Westpac Nowcast Q3 2026_ Second Estimate (2026-10-21)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Westpac Nowcast Q3 2026_ Second Estimate (2026-10-21).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-21

### Page 1

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
WESTPAC NOWCAST Q3
2026: SECOND ESTIMATE
Growth falters through the quarter
16 October 2026
Key points
• The second estimate from Westpac-Now
points to GDP growth of around +0.5%qtr
in Q3 2026 and 2.1%yr. This is slightly lower
than the first estimate of +0.6%qtr. This

still represents a slight step up in growth
from Q2 2026 but masks some loss of
momentum over the course of the quarter.
• Our Monthly Activity Index deteriorated
noticeably in August and September.
Weakness has become increasingly
broad-based, with labour and business
condition indicators among the
largest drags and relatively few areas
providing a meaningful offset.
• The economy is coming under increasing
pressure as the cumulative impact of
higher interest rates combines with

renewed disruptions in global energy
markets and a deepening housing
downturn. With another rate hike likely in
November, these pressures are expected
to build further heading into 2027.
• Overall, the economy is becoming
increasingly reliant on the upswing in
business investment to support growth.
This narrow base of growth has left the
balance of risks less favourable. Our central
case remains one of slowing rather than
stalling. Westpac-Now’s current model

dynamics point to growth moderating
to around +0.4%qtr in Q4 2026. Our
formal forecasts have growth slowing
to a softer +0.2%qtr pace (see here).
• Our final Westpac-Now estimate for Q3
2026 will be published on 20 November.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
In November 2025, we introduced Westpac-Now (see here),
a continuously-updated measure of current activity in the
Australian economy. Westpac-Now draws on more than
60 high frequency economic and financial variables, using
advanced statistical techniques to provide a timely pulse

check on economic momentum. It is the first Australian
model to incorporate internal banking data, enhancing its
predictive power.
This note provides the second estimate of GDP growth for the
September quarter 2026, using domestic and international
data released up to and including the September Labour Force
Survey (released 15 October 2026). Updates are published
monthly (see Appendix A for the release schedule).
Westpac Monthly Activity Index: activity falters as

headwinds mount
After showing tentative signs of improvement through the
second half of 2025, the Westpac Monthly Activity Index has
weakened markedly over 2026. The Index moved back into
negative territory in February and has declined further since.
While there were some signs of stabilisation around mid-year,
this proved to be short lived with the Index falling to –0.88 in
September, outside of the pandemic period, the weakest read
since 2013.
The economy is coming under pressure from multiple

fronts, with key headwinds coming from higher interest
rates, renewed disruptions to global energy markets and a
deepening in the housing downturn. Among households,
spending held up surprisingly well through the first half of the
year despite historically weak sentiment, but more recently
Growth momentum on shaky ground
spending outside of fuel has started to cool (see here). At
the same time, the labour market has continued to soften,
with unemployment reaching a cycle high of 4.7% (see here).

Meanwhile, the business sector is also showing signs of
softening, with sentiment deteriorating and conditions holding
at weak levels (see here). Business credit growth remains
a notable exception, although even here there are tentative
signs of easing (see here).
This deterioration is particularly evident in our heatmap.
Negative contributions have become both broader and more
pronounced through 2026, with the weakness intensifying
in August and September. Labour and business condition

indicators are now the largest sources of weakness, while
spending and confidence have also deteriorated materially.
Credit and capacity utilisation indicators have been
comparatively less negative, but there are currently few areas
providing a meaningful offset to the broader softening.
Our indicator heatmap decomposes changes in the Monthly
Activity Index into group-level contributions, allowing us to
assess which components are driving movements in activity.

For ease of interpretation, indicators are grouped into broad
categories (see the Appendix for further details). For example,
the labour market category includes both official ABS Labour
Force Survey outcomes and timelier survey-based measures of
labour demand and job advertisements.
Westpac-Now: Resilient growth outlook slowing not
stalling
Recall that Westpac-Now is a refined indicator that combines
our Monthly Activity Index with advanced econometric

techniques to estimate GDP growth. As previously noted,
the fit is not expected to be exact. Measured GDP includes
Neha Sharma
Economist

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
components that are not well captured by high-frequency
activity indicators, such as imputed rents for owner-occupiers,
housing-related ownership transfer costs, public and business
inventories, and depreciation.
To bridge monthly and quarterly data, we use a MIDAS

framework, allowing us to combine mixed-frequency inputs
and incorporate partial information for the current quarter
when producing GDP forecasts.
Based on current model estimates, Westpac-Now’s second
estimate for Q3 2026 points to GDP growth of 0.5%qtr
(with a 70% confidence interval of –0.1%qtr to 1.0%qtr)
and 2.1%yr, representing a slight downgrade from the first
estimate of +0.6%qtr but still a step up from the 0.4%qtr
(2.1%yr) outcome recorded in Q2 2026. While aggregate

growth appears to have held up through Q3, the underlying
picture became softer as the quarter progressed. At the
second decimal place the estimate is 0.46%qtr, leaving it
close to rounding down to 0.4%qtr.
With several headwinds now bearing on the economy
simultaneously, there is a growing risk that the slowdown in
activity becomes more pronounced than currently anticipated.
At the same time, the strength of the business investment
upswing provides an important counterweight that should

help cushion the broader slowdown.
Looking ahead, model dynamics suggest growth will tick
down to 0.4%qtr in Q4 2026 before picking up in Q1 2027.
However, these projections remain much more tentative,
given their reliance on projected inputs rather than realised
data. In addition, heightened uncertainty, particularly around
the renewed tensions in the Middle East conflict, means risks
around these forward estimates are larger than usual.
September Quarter data: a softer end to the quarter

Data released in the first month of the September quarter
indicated that the economy has picked up momentum relative
to the previous quarter, with our first nowcast estimating Q3
growth at 0.6%qtr, in line with the 0.6%qtr “pure forecast”
based solely on data available up to June 2026.
However, the flow of data since then shows downside risks to
growth have begun to materialise. Incorporating the August,
followed by the September (to date), data both yielded

nowcasts of 0.5%qtr (with a slight softening at the second
decimal place). This contrasts with Q2 2026, when weakness
emerged early in the quarter, in April, before subsequent data
pointed to stabilisation.
Note that monthly activity estimates are more uncertain
than normal when inflation is shifting. Faster price growth
can cause nominal indicators, such as consumer spending,
to rise more strongly even though ‘real’, inflation-adjusted
spending may be softening (and vice versa). The inclusion

of ‘real’ indicators, such as employment and banking
transaction volumes, helps mitigate some – but not all – of this
uncertainty.
Bottom line:
Westpac-Now points to GDP growth of around 0.5%qtr
(2.1%yr) in Q3 2026, suggesting aggregate growth held up
reasonably well through the quarter, although the flow of
data points to softer momentum as the quarter progressed.
The substantial upswing in business investment remains
an important source of support, helping to offset mounting

weakness elsewhere in the economy.
Looking ahead, the accumulation of headwinds is expected to
see growth slow further towards year-end, with Westpac-Now
currently pointing to growth of around 0.4%qtr in Q4 2026.
Downside risks around the outlook have increased, but for now
our central case remains one of slower growth rather than a
material downturn.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Appendix A: Westpac-Now Release Schedule (2026)
Going forward, Westpac Now will be published on the Friday following each ABS Labour Force Survey (LFS) release, at 11:30am AEDT/AEST (Canberra time).
Each release incorporates the latest labour market data alongside other high frequency indicators and provides an updated nowcast for quarterly GDP growth.

As the quarter progresses, each update should be interpreted as a progressively more complete estimate of that quarter’s economic performance.
Release date (Friday) LFS reference month Quarter focus Interpretation
20 November 2026 October 2026 Q3 2026 – final estimate Near complete read on Q3 activity
18 December 2026 November 2026 Q4 2026 – first estimate Initial assessment of Q4 momentum

なぜ企業は同じ情報を何度も提出 しなければ
ならないのか 
公的統計のデジタル化を問う①－負担軽減の「効果」

政策調査部 主任研究員 溝端 幹雄

[要約] 
 公的統計は、経済社会の実態を把握する上で重要な公共財である。しかし近年、回答
負担の重さなどを背景に、統計調査全体の回収率の低下が世界的な問題となっており、
日本も例外ではない。世帯系調査だけでなく企業系調査でも回収率が 低下し、標本の
偏りや推計精度の悪化を通じて、統計の信頼性と制度の持続可能性を脅かしている。 
 企業、とりわけ一定規模以上の企業は、複数の基幹統計調査・一般統計調査の対象と
なり得る。売上高・従業者数・設備投資などの類似項目でも、定義、対象範囲、対象
期間、締め切りが異なり、調査ごとに確認・加工する必要がある。負担は専任の統計
担当を置きにくい中小企業ほど固定費的に重く、統計だけでなく企業の「レポーティ
ング全体」を見据えれば、削減余地は大きい。 
 負担軽減の第一歩は測定 である。日本でも「官民の統計コスト削減」に取り組んだが 、
その後、同じ枠組みによる政府横断的な継続計測は確認できない。海外では、 標準コ
ストモデルや法制度に基づき、行政手続・政府の情報収集に伴う負担の計測が行われ
ている。日本の次期第 V 期公的統計基本計画では計測を制度に組み込み、 「測って減ら
す」PDCA を回すべきである。 
 デジタル化による負担軽減の効果は、①調査の電子化（オンライン回答） 、②調査自体
の削減（行政記録・企業データの連携） 、③調査の代替（民間・オルタナティブデータ
の活用）の3 段階で整理できる。生成AI は各段階を横断的に後押しする。さらに、企
業を、統計を「出す側」から「使う側」へと位置づけ直し、速報化・詳細化や統計デ
ータの安全な利活用などの便益を返す設計が鍵となる。

### Page 2

2 / 9

はじめに ― 制度の持続可能性と企業の報告者負担 
公的統計は、経済社会の実態を把握する上で重要な公共財である。景気判断、予算編成、社
会保障や地域政策の設計、企業の事業計画に至るまで、その基盤には公的統計がある。しかし
近年、回答の負担の重さなどを背景に、統計調査全体の回収率の低下が世界的な問題となって
おり、日本も例外ではない。 
欧米では、個人・世帯を対象とした世帯系調査だけでなく、企業・事業所を対象とした企業
系調査でも回収率が低下している 1。日本でも、厚生労働省「国民生活基礎調査」 （大規模調査）
の世帯票回収率は1986 年の95.7％から2025 年には62.6％へ低下した。財務省「年次別法人企
業統計調査」2も 2001 年度の 81.8％から 2025 年度の 76.2％へ低下し、特に資本金1,000 万円
未満の小規模法人では72.2％から60.5％への低下が目立つ（図表 1）。

図表 1 統計調査の回収率は低下傾向（ 左：世帯系調査、右：企業系調査 ）

（注）世帯系調査の回収率は「国民生活基礎調査」 （大規模調査） 、企業系調査の回収率は「年次別法人企業統
計調査」 。 
（出所）厚生労働省・財務省より大和総研作成

回収率の低下は、標本の偏りや推計精度の悪化を通じて統計の信頼性に直結するため 、対策
は喫緊の課題である。その背景には後述するように、企業にとって公的統計調査の報告者負担
が重いことがある。この負担は企業の生産性を押し下げる要因となっており看過できない。 
統計を作成する側でも、調査員の減少・高齢化や予算・人員の制約が強まる 3一方、デジタ
ル時代には速報性・詳細性・網羅性への需要が拡大している（図表 2） 。この需給ギャップを埋
める鍵がデジタル化である。

1 世帯系調査の回収率低下については、溝端幹雄［2025］「デジタル時代で変容を迫られる公的統計：回収率
低下やニーズ拡大を受けて大きな曲がり角に」大和総研レポート（2025 年3 月27 日）を参照されたい。 
2 趨勢的な回収率の動向を見るため、長期時系列データを入手できる金融業・保険業を除く系列を用いた。 
3 総務省政策統括官（統計制度担当）［2026］「調査員調査について」統計法等に関する研究会（第 4 回）資
料 1-2、令和8 年7 月。

図表 2 公的統計を取り巻く需給ギャップ

（出所）大和総研作成

政府は2007 年に全面改正された統計法（平成19 年法律第53 号）に基づき、おおむね5 年ご
とに公的統計基本計画（以下、基本計画）を策定してきた。現行の第 IV 期基本計画は 2027 年
度に計画期間を終えるため、次期の第 V 期基本計画に向けた検討が今後進むと見込まれる。デ
ジタル時代の公的統計の在り方があらためて問われる。 
今回のレポートでは、とりわけ多くの公的統計への報告に直面する企業の負担の重さという
観点から、前編として統計調査の実態及びデジタル化による「効果」を整理する。そして後編
となる次回のレポートでは、統計調査のデジタル化実現に向けた「課題」と次期の第 V 期基本
計画への提言を行う。

企業が直面する報告者負担の実態 
企業、とりわけ一定規模以上の企業は、複数の基幹統計調査 ・一般統計調査 4の対象となり
得る。例えば、 「経済センサス」 「経済構造実態調査」 「法人企業統計調査」 「毎月勤労統計調査」
「商業動態統計調査」 「サービス産業動態統計調査」 「経済産業省企業活動基本調査」 「科学技術
研究調査」などである（図表3）。 
問題は調査の「数」だけではない。売上高・従業者数・設備投資などの類似項目でも、定義、
対象範囲、対象期間、締め切りが異なり、企業は調査ごとに確認・加工する必要がある。紙・
電子・各府省のシステムが併存し、部署をまたぐ名寄せや転記も生じる5。 
負担が構造的に生じる背景には、日本が各府省でそれぞれ所管分野の統計を作成する「分散
型統計機構」を採用していることがある。調査が府省・分野ごとに個別に設計されるため、府

4 国の行政機関が作成する統計のうち総務大臣が指定する特に重要な統計を「基幹統計」、それ以外を「一般
統計」と呼び、基幹統計を統計調査により作成する「基幹統計調査」とそれ以外の「一般統計調査」の 2 つが
ある。前者の基幹統計調査には報告義務があり、統計法では基幹統計調査の報告（回答）を求められた者が、
報告を拒んだり虚偽の報告をしたりすることを禁止しており（第 13 条）、これらに違反した者に対して、50
万円以下の罰金が定められている（第 61 条）。しかし、罰則の適用は実態として形骸化しているとの報告が
以前よりあり（総務省［2008］「非協力者への処罰（罰則の適用について）」統計委員会基本計画部会第 4 ワ
ーキンググループ会合（第 8 回）配布資料 資料 3-3、平成20 年5 月13 日）、現在の関心は統計行政への協力
を得るための対策に移っている。 
5 総務省［2017］「統計調査の負担感・重複感の実態に関する調査（実施結果）」総務省政策統括官（統計基
準担当）、平成 29 年 5 月 12 日。 
需要の拡大（統計に求められるもの） 供給の制約（統計を作る側の事情）
速報性 ― より速く 統計調査員の減少・高齢化
詳細性 ― より細かく（地域・業種別 等） 回収率の低下による精度の悪化

省横断の調整が不十分な場合、類似項目の重複や定義の不統一が生じやすい。また、統計法に
より報告義務が課される調査がある一方、回答負担を府省横断で継続的に軽減する仕組みはな
お発展途上にある。

図表 3 企業が回答を求められる主な公的統計調査

（注）主要な調査の例であり網羅的ではない。調査対象・実施周期・回答義務は調査ごとに異なる。 
（出所）総務省・経済産業省・財務省・厚生労働省より大和総研作成

経団連の 2021 年の会員企業アンケート 6では、7 割以上が（税・社会保険・登記などの）行
政記録情報の活用は統計調査の回答負担軽減につながると回答した。重複報告の解消は、回答
の遅延や記入誤りを抑え、回収率や統計精度の向上にも寄与する可能性がある。 
しかも、これらの調査は年間を通じて絶えず到来する。毎月の月次調査、毎四半期の四半期
調査、年に一度の年次調査、そして数年ごとの周期調査が重なり合い、企業の統計担当は「常
にどれかの締め切りに追われている」状態に置かれやすい（図表 4） 。負担は特定の時期に集中
するのではなく、通年で恒常的に発生する点にも留意が必要だ。

### Page 5

1

5 / 9

報告者負担の「構造」を分解する 
負担軽減を論じる前に、企業の報告者負担が何から成るかを分解しておきたい。負担は調査
の「数」だけで測れるものではなく、少なくとも次の3 つの層から成る（図表5）。

図表 5 企業の報告者負担の 3 層構造

（出所）大和総研作成

第一に、直接費用である。調査票の記入・確認・提出に要する時間と人件費であり、最も見
えやすい負担である。回答項目が多く、専門的であるほど費用は膨らむ。 
第二に、間接的・心理的コストである。複数部署にまたがるデータの名寄せ、社内の稟議・
決裁、多数の締め切りの管理、疑義照会への対応など、記入以外に生じる負担であり、直接の
記入時間だけでは捉えにくい大きな負担となる場合がある 7。オンライン化で記入時間が減っ
ても、この層が残れば体感負担は下がりにくい。 
第三に、逆進性である。専任の統計担当を置きにくい中小企業ほど、負担は固定費的に重く
のしかかる。負担の重さは回答の遅延・記入誤り・未回答を招く。前掲の図表 1 右でも小規模
法人ほど回収率が低いことが示されているが、その背景には報告負担に加え、回答体制や調査
への認知など複数の要因が考えられる。負担軽減は、中小を含む幅広い企業の協力を得るため
にも重要である。 
企業の情報提出は、統計調査に加え、財務報告、ESG（環境・社会・ガバナンス） ・サステナ
ビリティ開示、税申告などにも及ぶ。同種データを別々の様式で提出する重複を踏まえれば、
様式・定義の標準化やデータ連携による削減余地は大きい。統計だけでなく企業の「レポーテ
ィング全体」を見据えることが、デジタル化の効果を最大化する。

負担は「測る」ことが可能 ― まず見える化を 
負担軽減の第一歩は測定である。日本でも統計改革の一環として「官民の統計コスト削減」

7 日本経済団体連合会［2021］や総務省［2017］でも、「追加的な作業が必要となる（他部署へのデータ提供
要請等）」「他部門・支店・事業所等へのデータ提供依頼が必要」という点で負担を感じるという回答が最も
多い。 
層 内容 軽減の着眼点
① 直接費用 記入・確認・提出の時間と人件費 電子化・自動転記による入力削減
② 間接的・心理的コスト 名寄せ・稟議・締切管理・疑義対応 共通認証・様式標準化・once-only
③ 逆進性 中小ほど固定費的に重い 行政記録での代替、簡易な回答手段
（横断）レポーティング全体 統計＋財務(XBRL)＋ESG＋税務の重複 定義・様式の標準化とデータ連携

### Page 6

1

6 / 9

に取り組んだ 8が、その後、同じ枠組みによる政府横断的な継続計測は確認できない。共通の
物差しがなければ、議論は感覚論に流れやすい。 
海外では負担計測が制度化されている（ 図表 6）。標準コストモデル（Standard Cost 
Model：SCM）は、情報提供義務への対応にかかる行政手続コスト を、「人件費単価× 所要時間
×対象数×頻度」等により金額換算する手法で、オランダ、デンマーク、英国などが活用して
きた。米国も文書業務削減法（Paperwork Reduction Act：PRA）の下で、行政管理予算局（OMB）
が報告負担時間を集計・公表している。負担の可視化により、施策の優先順位づけと進捗管理
が可能になる。 
日本でも、次期第 V 期基本計画では計測を制度に組み込み、 「測って減らす」PDCAを回すべ
きである。一企業当たりの回答調査数、回答時間、準備・社内調整を含む費用、重複項目数な
どを、統一した定義で企業規模別に把握する 9。これがデジタル化施策の評価と説明責任の基
盤になる。

図表 6 行政手続・統計報告の負担を計測する国内外の枠組み

（出所）総務省、オランダ政府、デンマーク政府、英国政府、米国行政管理予算局資料より大和総研作成

オンライン回答の到達点と限界 
総務省によると 10、オンライン導入率（オンライン回答手段を導入した調査の割合）は2026
年 3 月末で 94.3％に達した。一方、現行の第 IV 期基本計画が掲げる基幹統計調査のオンライ
ン回答率（実際の回答に占めるオンライン回答の割合）の目標（企業系8 割以上、世帯系 5 割
以上）に対し、2025 年度は企業系62.3％、世帯系28.8％にとどまる。選択肢が用意されても、

8 総務省政策統括官（統計制度担当）［2021］「官民の統計コスト削減に係る最終フォローアップ結果」令和
3 年9 月。さらに、統計を含む行政手続全般の負担を測定したものでは、規制改革推進会議行政手続部会
［2018］「行政手続コスト削減に向けて（見直し結果と今後の方針）」（第 30 回規制改革推進会議 資料 3-
2、平成 30 年4 月24 日、令和元年7 月29 日改定）が公表されている。これは、各省庁が事業者に行政手続の
申請等に要した作業時間を聴取し、その作業時間を人件費に換算して試算したものである。 
9 政府は 2017 年の調査において、国の統計調査の1 企業当たりの年間依頼件数を把握しており、資本金等100
億円以上の企業では 1 企業当たり年間で34.2 件（回答企業ベース）と報告している（総務省［2017］「統計
調査の負担感・重複感の実態に関する調査（実施結果）」総務省政策統括官（統計基準担当）、平成 29 年 5
月 12 日）。しかし、こうした把握を継続的にフォローアップしてはいない。 
10 総務省［2026］「公的統計作成等への AI 等のデジタル技術の活用及びAI 等によるデータ分析に資する統計
データの提供について」統計法等に関する研究会（第 5 回）資料 2 背景資料、令和8 年7 月 29 日。統計調査
数は、令和 8 年（2026 年）3 月末現在で回答調査客体数が確定している直近の基幹統計調査及び一般統計調査
の数（周期調査等を含む）。 
主体 枠組み 特徴
日本 「官民の統計コスト削減」（総務省） 実施者・報告者・利用者のコストを把握（令和3年に最終フォローアップ）
オランダ・デンマーク・英国 等 標準コストモデル（SCM）／削減目標 負担を金額換算し、定量的な削減目標を設定
米国 文書業務削減法（PRA）／OMB・OIRA 連邦政府の情報収集ごとに回答時間・費用を推計し、承認・公表

利用が十分進んでいるとは限らない。 
その背景には、回答者の高齢化やPC 利用率の低さ、設問の多さ、複数部署での分担、紙の方
が一覧性・共有性に優れること、操作・システム上の制約等がある（図表 7） 。単に画面を用意
するだけでは解消しない。 
つまり、オンライン化＝電子化は「入力チャネルを紙から画面に替える」ものにとどまり、
同じ項目を繰り返し入力させる構造そのものは残る。ここに電子化の限界がある。負担を根本
から軽減するには、次で述べる「削減」 「代替」の段階へ進む必要がある。

図表 7 オンライン回答の現状・目標・伸び悩みの要因

（出所）総務省「令和 6 年度・令和 7 年度統計法施行状況報告」より大和総研作成

デジタル化による「効果」 ― 負担軽減の 3 段階 
負担を根本から軽減するデジタル化の効果は、報告負担を段階的に引き下げる形で整理でき
る。すなわち、①調査の電子化（オンライン回答） 、②調査自体の削減（行政記録・企業デー
タの連携） 、③調査の代替（民間・オルタナティブデータの活用）の3 段階である（図表 8）。
①から③へ進むほど、負担軽減の効果は大きくなるが、実現の難易度も高まる。先述したとお
り①の電子化には限界があり、以下では②と③に重点を置いて述べる。

図表 8 デジタル化による報告者負担軽減の 3 段階

（出所）大和総研作成

① 電子化 ― 入力チャネルの電子化 
電子回答は郵送・記入の手間を減らし、入力チェックで誤りも抑える。 「Gビズ ID」11等の共

11 G ビズID は、すべての事業者を対象とする共通認証システムである。アカウントを作成すると、一つのID
とパスワードで複数の行政サービスにログインでき、補助金申請、社会保険手続、許認可に関する申請など、
業務上の各種電子手続に利用できる。詳しくは、デジタル庁「G ビズ ID」を参照。 
項目 内容
導入率 94.3％（オンライン調査を導入した統計調査の割合、2026年3月末時点）
第IV期基本計画の目
標 基幹統計調査のオンライン回答率を、企業系8割以上・世帯系5割以上（2027年度までの5年間）
伸び悩みの要因 回答者の高齢化・PC利用率の低さ、紙の稟議ニーズ、複数部署での分担回答、セキュリティ面の制約　等
段階 考え方 主な手段・事例
① 電子化 調査票をオンライン化し入力を効率化 政府統計オンライン調査総合窓口、調査対象者IDの統合将来的にはgBizID等との認証連携も検討余地がある
② 削減 既に保有する情報で代替し「そもそも聞かない」 行政記録の統計利用（英VAT月次指標）、企業システム連携・SBR/XBRL、北欧レジスター
③ 代替 民間・オルタナティブデータで統計を代替・補完 POS・スキャナー、キャッシュレス決済データ 等

### Page 8

1

8 / 9

通認証を用いれば認証の手間も軽減できる。しかし、同じ項目を何度も入力させる構造は残る
ため、負担の根本的削減には次の段階が必要である。

② 削減 ― 「そもそも聞かない」 （once-only） 
税・社会保険・登記などの行政記録情報を統計に転用できれば、企業に「そもそも聞かない」
で済む。英国国家統計局（ONS）は付加価値税申告データを短期経済指標に利用している。ま
た、行政が一度取得した情報の再提出を求めない「once-only 原則」は EU のデジタル行政にお
ける重要な原則とされ、対象手続について域内実装が進められている。 
会計・ERP（統合基幹業務システム）・給与などの企業システムから、 API（アプリケーショ
ン・プログラミング・インターフェース）連携や XBRL12で標準様式のデータを提出できれば転
記負担は減る。オランダや豪州の標準ビジネスレポーティング（Standard Business 
Reporting：SBR）は、項目定義と様式を標準化し、複数行政機関への報告を効率化する。さら
に北欧諸国の一部では行政記録を統合した登録簿（レジスター）を統計基盤とし、報告者負担
の抜本的軽減と作成効率化を両立している13。

③ 代替 ― 民間・オルタナティブデータの活用 
POS・スキャナーデータやキャッシュレス決済データ等 は、統計の一部を代替・補完 し、速
報性と詳細性を高める（図表 9） 。日本でも消費者物価指数の一部に POS データが導入されてい
る。また、商業動態統計調査の家電大型専門店分野では POS データ等を用いた提出方法が導入
されている。企業系調査を民間データで代替すれば報告負担を減らせるが、代表性・継続性を
踏まえ、公的統計と補完的に用いる必要がある。

図表 9 国内外のビッグデータ・行政記録の活用事例

（出所）総務省、経済産業省、英国国家統計局（ONS） 、オランダ統計局（CBS） 、米国BEA より大和総研作成

12 XBRL（Extensible Business Reporting Language：拡張可能な事業報告言語）とは、XML（Extensible 
Markup Language）を基礎として、企業の財務・事業報告情報を作成、流通および利用しやすくするために標
準化されたデータ記述言語のこと。 
13 なお、総務省でも現在、事業所母集団データベースの事業所 ID（共通事業所コード）を用いて、報告者の負
担軽減につながる重複排除や高度な分析の推進に関する議論が行われている（総務省「経済統計の基盤整備・
事業所母集団データベースの機能高度化について」統計法等に関する研究会（第 2 回）資料2、2026 年6 月19
日）。 
国 公表元 統計名 データの種類／対象
日本 総務省 消費者物価指数(CPI) POSデータ（家電 等）、ウェブスクレイピング（航空運賃・宿泊料 等）
日本 経済産業省 商業動態統計・丁2調査 POSデータ等による提出・組替集計（家電大型専門店の商品販売額等）
英国 ONS VAT flash estimates（売上高DI） 行政記録情報（HMRCのVAT申告に基づく売上高データ）
オランダ 統計局 消費者物価指数(CPI) スキャナーデータ、ウェブスクレイピング（スーパー、衣料品 等）
米国 BEA Near Real-Time Spending（実験的推計、2024年終了） クレジット・デビット・ギフトカード取引データ

### Page 9

1

9 / 9

生成AI がもたらす「第 4 の層」による横断的な業務支援 
前掲図表 8 で示した3 段階に加え、生成AI は報告者負担の軽減に新たな層を開きつつある。
作業を人手から機械へ移す点で、電子化・削減・代替の各段階を横断的に後押しする。 
統計の報告側（企業・事業所）では、調査票の下書きの自動生成、社内データからの自動転
記・項目マッピング（調査票の項目と対応させること）、用語・定義の自動解説、疑義照会へ
の一次的な自動応答などが期待される。企業が「何を、どの定義で書けばよいか」を調べる手
間を減らし、間接・心理コストの層に効く。 
統計の作成側では、記入内容のAI 審査、産業・職業の自動符号づけ、自由記述の自動分類に
より、審査・集計の効率と正確性を高められる。導入には、精度検証、説明可能性、機密情報
管理を含む運用基準が必要である。 
ただし、生成AI には誤りや品質のばらつきがあり、最終確認は人が担う必要がある。機密情
報の管理と一体で導入し、3 段階の各施策を補強する道具として位置づけるべきである。

「出す側」から「使う側」へ ― 便益の可視化 
負担軽減を持続させる最後の鍵は、企業に協力の見返り（便益）を返すことにある。負担を
求める一方でメリットがなければ、協力は続かない。デジタル化は、提出されたデータを企業
に「返す」便益の設計を可能にする。企業を、統計を「出す側」から「使う側」へと位置づけ
直す発想である。 
・速報化・詳細化：統計をより早く・細かく提供し、企業が自社の景況判断・投資判断に使
いやすくする。 
・統計データの安全な利活用：オンサイト施設やリモートアクセスによる匿名化されたミク
ロデータの研究利用を拡充するとともに、企業が自社の状況を業界平均や同規模企業の集計値
と比較できるベンチマーク情報を提供する。 
・政策の質の向上：EBPM（証拠に基づく政策立案）を通じて、企業の事業環境そのものを改
善する。 
負担論を便益論へ転換することが、企業の継続的な協力の基盤となる。オンライン化だけで
は足りない。once-only と代替（民間・オルタナティブデータの活用）、生成AI、そして便益の
還元をあわせて初めて、企業の負担は根本から軽減され、統計の持続可能性が高まる。後編で
は、これらを阻む課題と、現行の第IV 期基本計画が終了する 2027 年度を見据えた次期第 V 期
基本計画への提言を示す。 
以上
